## Page 1


CONTINUOUS HOMOLOGY OF TOPOLOGICAL PERIODIC
HOMOLOGY OF COMPLEX COBORDISM
SVERRE LUNØE–NIELSEN AND JOHN ROGNES
Abstract. We determine the continuous mod p homology of the topological
periodic homology TP(MU) of the complex cobordism spectrum, as a graded
algebra with Steenrod operations. The answer is given in terms of an explicit
and purely algebraic construction C+, analogous to Singer’s construction R+.
Its Ext-algebra provides the E2-term for a multiplicative Adams-type spectral
sequence converging strongly to the homotopy of p-completed TP(MU).

1. Introduction
Let p be a prime. We determine the continuous mod p homology Hc
∗(TP(MU))
of the topological periodic homology of the complex cobordism spectrum, as a
graded algebra with Steenrod operations.
Its Ext-algebra provides the starting
page for a multiplicative Adams-type spectral sequence converging strongly to
π∗TP(MU)∧
p .
1.1. Context. For E1 ring spectra B, the p-complete algebraic K-theory K(B)∧
p
[EKMM97] and its close approximation, the topological cyclic homology TC(B)∧
p


## Page 3


TOPOLOGICAL PERIODIC HOMOLOGY OF COMPLEX COBORDISM
3
[BHM93], [DGM13], capture p-primary arithmetic information about B. In the
case B = S these invariants are related to the geometric topology of highly-
connected compact manifolds [WJR13], while for B = Z they are related to global
and local number theory [Tat63], [Kur92], [RW00]. Interpolating between these,
we have the complex cobordism spectrum MU [Mil60] and the truncated Brown–
Peterson spectra BP⟨n⟩of heights 0 < n < ∞[JW73]. We can view S →MU as
a Hopf–Galois extension [Rog08], with MU being close enough to S that one can
approach K(S) or TC(S) by descent from K(MU) or TC(MU) [DR18], but far
enough from S that π∗(MU) remains manageable. We therefore view the E∞ring
spectrum MU as an important intermediary, bridging between S and Z, and we
seek to understand the homotopy-theoretic information captured by K(MU) and
TC(MU), with the expectation that it will shed light on S, K(S) and TC(S).
1.2. A homological approach. Waldhausen’s perspective [Wal84] on the Quillen–
Lichtenbaum conjectures, and the calculations for BP⟨1⟩in [AR02], motivated the
so-called redshift conjectures predicting that K(BP⟨n⟩) and TC(BP⟨n⟩) have tele-
scopic height n + 1. These were confirmed by Hahn–Wilson in [HW22], playing a
role in the disproof by Burklund–Hahn–Levy–Schlank [BHLS] of Ravenel’s tele-
scope conjecture. However, this bound on telescopic height also means that one
cannot directly detect height ≥n + 2 phenomena in TC(BP⟨n⟩), and must there-
fore expect to go to TC(MU) to gain information about these for all n.
The
TC-calculations for BP⟨n⟩are manageable [AKAC+25] because they can be car-
ried out with coefficients in finite spectra of type n + 1, but for MU no such finite
type coefficients are available, and a direct calculation of TC(MU) in terms of
homotopy groups appears to be out of reach [Rog20]. A homological approach to
topological cyclic homology [AR05], [BR05] instead endeavors to first determine the
mod p homology H∗TC(MU) as a right A -module algebra, or equivalently as a
left A∗-comodule algebra, where A and A∗denote the mod p Steenrod algebra and
its dual, respectively, and then to approach the complexity of π∗TC(MU) through
the multiplicative Adams spectral sequence
Es,t
2
= Exts,t

p .
Having access to the algebra structure in this spectral sequence is likely to be
essential for any detailed calculations.
Using the Nikolaus–Scholze formalism [NS18], we are considering the cyclo-
tomic spectrum X := H ∧THH(MU), where H := HFp denotes the mod p
Eilenberg–MacLane spectrum with the trivial cyclotomic structure. Its topological
cyclic homology TC(X) ≃H ∧TC(MU) [CMM21, Def. 2.2, Thm. 2.7] then has
homotopy π∗TC(X) ∼= H∗TC(MU), and comes equipped with a natural action
by the Steenrod algebra, arising from graded self-maps of H. In this paper, we
shall determine the topological periodic homology TP(X) = (H ∧THH(MU))tT,
where T is the circle group acting in the standard way on THH(MU). We re-
fer to its homotopy π∗TP(X) = Hc
∗TP(MU) as the continuous homology of
TP(MU), which also arises naturally [LNR12, Def. 4.7] as the limit of the ho-
mologies of a tower of bounded-below spectra with homotopy limit the T-Tate
construction on THH(MU), in a manner that is compatible with the right A -
actions.
The corresponding left A∗-coaction takes values in a completed tensor
product A∗b⊗Hc
∗TP(MU), formed with respect to a specified filtration on the
continuous homology, yielding what we call a complete coaction.

to C+(M∗; σ), making the latter a relatively bounded below complete, Hausdorff
and exhaustively filtered right A -module algebra.

p .
A completely analogous theorem holds for the Brown–Peterson spectrum BP,
with H∗BP = P(¯ξk | k ≥1) and H∗THH(BP) ∼= P(¯ξk | k ≥1) ⊗E(σ¯ξk | k ≥1),
with essentially the same proof.

as a right A -module algebra when (M∗, σ) is a differential graded right A -module
algebra.
1.11. Outline of proof. To prove the main theorem, it remains to construct
the relatively bounded below complete filtered right A -module morphism ΘMU
from (1.9), in such a way that it is an isomorphism of differential graded alge-
bras. At the less structured level of topological right A -modules and continuous
homomorphisms, we already achieved the first task in [LNR11, Thm. 2.2], taking
ΘMU = Φ−1
MU. In order to also account for multiplicative structure, without getting
confounded by the many possible tensor products for topological vector spaces, we
shall reprove this result at the filtered level, and can now give a simpler argument
for the pro-isomorphisms involved.

2. Filtered modules, comodules and algebras
Let A ∗denote the mod p Steenrod algebra [Ste62], equipped with the usual
product ϕ: A ∗⊗A ∗→A ∗, coproduct ψ: A ∗→A ∗⊗A ∗and conjugation
χ: A ∗→A ∗. We often write A instead of A ∗. We write A∗for the dual [Mil58]
of A , and also let ϕ, ψ and χ denote the product, coproduct and conjugation of
A∗, respectively.
As an algebra, A∗= P(¯ζk | k ≥1) with |¯ζk| = 2k −1 for p = 2, while A∗=
E(¯τk | k ≥0) ⊗P(¯ξk | k ≥1) with |¯τk| = 2pk −1 and |¯ξk| = 2pk −2 for p odd.
Here ¯ζk = χ(ζk) for p = 2, and ¯ξk = χ(ξk) and ¯τk = χ(τk) for p odd, denote the
conjugates of the usual generators. The mod 2 Steenrod algebra is generated by the
elements Sq2n for n ≥0. For p > 2, the Bockstein β together with the elements P pn
generate A . For each integer n ≥0, we let A (n) be the sub-Hopf algebra generated
by the elements Sq2n, Sq2n−1, . . . , Sq1 when p = 2, and by P pn−1, P pn−2, . . . , P 1, β
when p > 2.
Let C be a category. The category gr C of Z-graded objects in C consists of
Z-indexed families of objects and maps in C , i.e., is equal to the functor category
C Z where Z is considered to be a discrete category. We write |x| to indicate the
degree of a (homogeneous) object x in gr C . Limits and colimits in gr C are taken
degreewise.
Definition 2.1. Let R be a noetherian commutative ring. A graded R-module is
of finite type over R if it is finitely generated over R in each degree.
Let RMod be the category of left R-modules and R-linear homomorphisms. The
graded tensor product ⊗= ⊗R over R makes grRMod a symmetric monoidal cate-
gory, with unit R considered as a graded R-module concentrated in degree zero.
Let grRModbb denote the full subcategory of grRMod consisting of graded R-
modules that are bounded below. This means that for each M∗in grRModbb there
exists an integer k such that Mq = 0 for all q < k, i.e., that M∗is (k −1)-connected
and k-connective.

2.3. Filtered graded R-modules. Let R be a noetherian commutative ring, such
as Z, Z(p), Zp or Fp for a prime p. We let filgrRMod be the category consisting of
objects (P∗, {FnP∗}n∈Z) where P∗is a graded R-module, and
. . . ⊂Fn−1P∗⊂FnP∗⊂. . . ⊂P∗
is an ascending filtration of P∗. We often omit the filtration from the notation, and
simply say P∗instead of (P∗, {FnP∗}n∈Z). The morphisms of filgrRMod are the
filtration-preserving maps of graded R-modules.
We say that a morphism f : P∗→Q∗of filtered graded R-modules is strictly
filtration-preserving if f(FnP∗\ Fn−1P∗) ⊂FnQ∗\ Fn−1Q∗, or equivalently if
¯f : FnP∗/Fn−1P∗→FnQ∗/Fn−1Q∗is injective for each n. Note that a morphism
f : P∗→Q∗is strictly filtration-preserving if P∗has the pullback filtration, i.e., if
FnP∗= f −1FnQ∗for each n ∈Z.
We say that the filtration {FnP∗}n is exhaustive if colimn FnP∗= P∗, Hausdorff
if limn FnP∗= 0, and complete if Rlimn FnP∗= 0.
The following characterization of isomorphisms in filgrRMod is elementary.
Lemma 2.3. A morphism f : P∗→Q∗in filgrRMod is an isomorphism if and only
if f is an isomorphism in grRMod that also induces an isomorphism of quotients
P∗/FnP∗∼= Q∗/FnQ∗for every n.
□
Definition 2.4. We say that an ascending filtration of graded R–modules
. . . ⊂Fn−1P∗⊂FnP∗⊂. . . ⊂P∗
is relatively bounded below, or rbb for short, if there exists an integer k such that
P∗/FnP∗is (n+k)-connected for each n. In other words, FnPq = Pq for all n ≥q−k.

We let filrbbgrRMod be the full subcategory of filgrRMod consisting of objects
(P∗, {FnP∗}n∈Z) where P∗is a graded (unbounded) R-module, and {FnP∗}n is a
relatively bounded below filtration of P∗.
Note that any relatively bounded below filtration is necessarily exhaustive since
any given homogeneous submodule Pq is eventually contained in some FnP∗.
We can identify grRModbb with the full subcategory of filrbbgrRMod consisting
of bounded below graded R-modules M∗having the discrete filtration given by
FnM∗= M∗for all n ≥0 and FnM∗= 0 otherwise. If M∗is k-connective, then the
discrete filtration has the property that M∗/FnM∗is (n + k)-connected for each n.

3. The G-Tate construction
Definition 3.1. A spectrum X is bounded below if π∗(X) is bounded below as
a graded abelian group. It is of finite type if π∗(X) is of finite type as a graded
Z-module.
Note that if X is a spectrum such that X/p is bounded below, then X/p is of
finite type if and only if the mod p homology group Hq(X; Fp) is finite for each
q ∈Z.
Equivariantly, we will work with G-spectra in the sense of [Sch18, Sec. 3.1], where
usually G is either the circle group T or any of its finite subgroups. As a model
for T we take the group of complex units of length one.
Let G be a closed subgroup of the circle group T, and X any spectrum with
G-action. The G-Tate construction on X is the fixed point spectrum
XtG =
g
EG ∧F(EG+, X)
G .
Here EG = S(∞C) is a free and contractible G-CW space, and g
EG = S∞C is
the mapping cone of the collapse map EG+ →S0. Since G acts freely on EG,
the genuinely G-equivariant homotopy type of F(EG+, X) depends only on X as
a naive G-spectrum. In particular it makes sense to form G-fixed points as stated.
Assigning the G-Tate construction XtG to a spectrum X with G-action defines
a lax symmetric monoidal functor on the homotopy category of G-spectra, with
pairing
(3.1)
µX,Y : XtG ∧Y tG −→(X ∧Y )tG ,
for any G-spectra X and Y , see [HR24, Sec. 6.2], and unit
(3.2)
η: S −→StG ,
given by composing the map S →SG given by the tom Dieck splitting with the
map Rh ◦Γ1 : SG →ShG →StG.

To prove the claim, note first that since j is filtration-preserving and surjective,
it induces surjections of filtration quotients. Secondly, since the Tate filtration of
π∗XtG is the pullback of the kernel filtration of limn π∗XtG[n] via the homomor-
phism j, it follows that j induces injections of filtration quotients. Thus, j induces
isomorphisms of filtration quotients.
□

Lemma 3.4. Suppose that X is bounded below. The Greenlees–May Tate spectral

(1) R ˆE∞= 0.

(2) G = T and π∗(X) is finite in each degree.
(3) G is finite cyclic and π∗(X) is finitely generated in each degree.
(4) The spectral sequence collapses at a finite stage.
Note that strong convergence of the Greenlees–May Tate spectral sequence implies
that the Tate filtration of π∗XtG is complete Hausdorff and exhaustive.
Proof. For bounded-below X, (3.4) is a half-plane spectral sequence with entering
differentials, and the condition R ˆE∞= 0 ensures that the spectral sequence is
strongly convergent by [Boa99, Thm. 7.3]. The lemma follows since any one of the
conditions (2)–(4) implies (1).
□

isomorphic by the short exact sequence of Lemma 3.2.

We summarize some of the conclusions in this subsection.
Proposition 3.11. The assignment X 7→Hc

limit Adams spectral sequence with with the cup product in algebraic Ext-groups,
via the isomorphism from Subsection 4.4.

5.1. The mod p homology of the extended power construction. For any
subgroup G ⊂Σp of the symmetric group, the G-extended power construction on
a spectrum B is the homotopy orbit spectrum
DG(B) = EG ⋉G B∧p .
Lemma 5.2 ([BMMS86, Cor. I.2.3]). For any spectrum B and any subgroup G ⊂
Σp of the symmetric group, there is a natural isomorphism
H∗(DG(B)) ∼= H∗(G; H∗(B)⊗p) ,
where G permutes the p copies of H∗(B).
□

Choose B to be a homogeneous vector space basis for H∗(B), so that Hq(B) is
spanned by Bq for each q ∈Z. Assuming G = Cp, we follow [BMMS86, §II.5]. The
mod p homology of the Cp-extended power construction splits additively as
H∗(DCp(B)) ∼= Fp{e0 ⊗x1 ⊗· · · ⊗xp} ⊕Fp{ej ⊗x⊗p | j ≥0} ,
where the xi and x range over B, the xi are not all equal, and only one repre-
sentative is taken from each Cp-orbit of the tensors x1 ⊗· · · ⊗xp. The grading is
determined by |ej| = j. In particular, H∗(BCp) = Fp{ej | j ≥0}.
In the formulas that follow, we will use the convention that any term involving
ej for j < 0 should be read as 0.

mod p homology of the Cp-extended power construction on B can be found in

It will follow from Proposition 6.10 that (6.3) is an isomorphism whenever B/p is

Miller observed [BMMS86, §II.5] that if one applies mod p cohomology to the
tower of extended powers (6.1) when G = Σp, the colimit of the resulting left A -
modules is isomorphic to a desuspension of the Σp-version of the algebraic Singer
construction R+(H∗(B)) on the left A -module H∗(B). See [Sin81], [LS82]. This
fact motivates the following definition.
Definition 6.1 ([LNR12, Def. 5.8]). The topological Singer construction on a spec-
trum B is the Cp-Tate construction
R+(B) = (B∧p)tCp .
Here B∧p is the p-fold smash power of B, considered as a spectrum with Cp acting
by cyclic permutation of the smash factors.
The calculations that identify the continuous cohomology of R+(B) with the
algebraic Singer construction on the left A -module H∗(B) were performed in
[LNR12], and explicit formulas for the action of the mod p Steenrod algebra on
Hc
∗(R+(B)) were derived by dualizing from cohomology to homology. These du-
alizations depended on the assumption that B/p is bounded below and of finite
type.
In the present work we revisit the topological Singer construction, since we need
to discuss the multiplicative structure of Hc
∗(R+(B)). Also, by working exclusively
in mod p homology we arrive at explicit formulas for the right action of A on
Hc
∗(R+(B)), without the assumption that B/p is of finite type.
Remark 6.2. Let B = S. The p-fold product map S∧p →S is a Cp-equivariant
equivalence, inducing an equivalence of spectra R+(S) = (S∧p)tCp ≃StCp. Propo-
sition 5.6 thus describes Hc
∗(R+(B)) as a filtered differential graded right A -module
algebra in the case of B = S.
6.1. The E-based Singer construction. Consider the case G = Cp, let E be
an E∞ring spectrum, and let M be an E-module spectrum. Then M ∧Ep is an
E-module with Cp acting by cyclic permutations on the smash factors, in the sym-
metric monoidal category of E-modules. The Cp-equivariant shuffle isomorphism
M ∧Ep ∧E N ∧Ep ∼= (M ∧E N)∧Ep gives the assignment M 7→M ∧Ep the structure
of a strong symmetric monoidal functor.
The E-based topological Singer construction on an E-module M is the Cp-Tate
construction
RE
+(M) = (M ∧Ep)tCp = (M ∧E · · · ∧E M)tCp .


## Page 52

SVERRE LUNØE–NIELSEN AND JOHN ROGNES
It is a lax symmetric monoidal endofunctor on the homotopy category of E-modules,
being the composition of a strong and a lax symmetric monoidal functor.
We will only be interested in the E-based Singer construction on induced E-
modules, i.e., those of the form M = E ∧B for B a spectrum.
We make the
following generalization of Definition 6.1.
Definition 6.3. The E-based topological Singer construction on a spectrum B is
the Cp-Tate construction
RE
+(E ∧B) = ((E ∧B)∧Ep)tCp .
Note that there is a natural equivalence of E-modules RE
+(E∧B) ≃(E∧B∧p)tCp.
The assignment B 7→E∧B is a strong symmetric monoidal functor, and it follows
that B 7→RE
+(E ∧B) is a lax symmetric monoidal functor from the homotopy
category of spectra to the homotopy category of E-modules.
Suppose temporarily that E = S. Associated to a spectrum B, the structure
map of the S-based topological Singer construction, now often known as the Tate

7. The homological Cp-Singer construction
Recall from Subsection 5.2 the structure of Hc
∗(StCp) ∼= ˆH−∗(Cp; Fp) as a graded
commutative Fp-algebra.
Definition 7.1. For a graded Fp-vector space M∗, let
r+(M∗) = Hc
∗(StCp) ⊗M∗,
and define the Tate filtration of r+(M∗) to be the ascending Hausdorff filtration
given by the span
Fnr+(M∗) = ⟨uitr ⊗x | −i −2r −|x|(p −1) ≤n, x ∈M∗⟩
for each n ∈Z.
The homological Singer construction on M∗is the completion
R+(M∗) = r+(M∗)∧
of r+(M∗) with respect to the Tate filtration.
Note that r+(H∗(B)) is the domain of the homomorphism ωB defined in Sub-
section 6.3.2.
The following lemma is elementary, and applies, in particular, for M = Fp.
Lemma 7.2. If M∗is bounded above, then the Tate filtration on r+(M∗) is discrete
in each degree and r+(M∗) = R+(M∗).
If M∗is bounded below, then the Tate filtration is relatively bounded below.
□

satisfies cq+2,j ≡−cq,j and is four-periodic in q. The lemma then follows for q ≥1
since c1,j = (−1)j+1α(0) = (−1)jm!, and c2,j ≡(−1)2(j+1)α(0)α(1) ≡−1.
□

There is a natural morphism ϵ: M∗→r+(M∗) defined for so-called algebraic
right A -modules M∗. We prove in Proposition 8.9 that when M∗= H∗(B) is the

Singer’s ϵ-homomorphism models the H-based Tate diagonal map.
Let M∗be a left A∗-comodule with structure map ν : M∗→A∗⊗M∗. Recall
from Subsection 2.2 that ν gives rise to a right action of A on M∗, with structure
map M∗⊗A →M∗adjoint to the composite
M∗

An Fp-linear homomorphism f : A →M∗is contained in the image of ν if and
only if f vanishes on all but finitely many elements a ∈A .
It follows that a
right A -module structure on M∗arises from a left A∗-comodule structure in the
way described above if and only if each element x ∈M∗has the property that
P s
∗(x) = 0 for all but finitely many integers s. A right A -module satisfying this
property will be called algebraic. An element x ∈M∗is algebraic if it is contained
in an algebraic A -submodule of M∗.
Thus, x ∈M∗is algebraic if and only if
P s
∗(x) = 0 for all but finitely many integers s.
Examples of algebraic right A -modules include modules that are bounded below,
and modules that are the mod p homology of spectra.
In contrast, the next lemma implies that r+(M∗) is essentially never an algebraic
right A -module. When M∗= Fp, we recall from (7.13)–(7.16) for B = S that the
right A -module structure on r+(Fp) is given by formulas (5.10)–(5.12).

Let Q∗be a right A -module. If the only algebraic element of Q∗is 0, we say
that Q∗is totally non-algebraic. Lemma 8.1 says that the quotient r+(Fp)/Fp{1} is
totally non-algebraic. If M∗is algebraic and Q∗is totally non-algebraic, then any
morphism f : M∗→Q∗must be trivial.
Lemma 8.2. If Q∗is a totally non-algebraic right A -module, then the tensor
product Q∗⊗N∗is totally non-algebraic for any right A -module N∗.
Proof. The degree filtration of N∗induces a filtration of Q∗⊗N∗which is exhaustive
and Hausdorff. Thus, for any non-trivial element z ∈Q∗⊗N∗, there exists an
integer q such that z ∈Q∗⊗N∗≤q, and such that z maps non-trivially to the
filtration quotient Q∗⊗N∗≤q/N∗≤q−1. It follows from the Cartan formula that
Q∗⊗N∗≤q/N∗≤q−1 is totally non-algebraic.
Therefore, z must be totally non-
algebraic. Since z was an arbitrary non-trivial element, the lemma follows.
□

Proposition 9.2. Let (M∗, σ) be a differential graded right A -module algebra. If
β∗acts trivially on M∗, then (r+(M∗), ¯σ) is a filtered differential graded right A -
module algebra and its completion (R+(M∗), ¯σ) is a complete differential graded
right A -module algebra. If M∗is bounded below, then these filtered algebras are
relatively bounded below (rbb).
Proof. By a direct calculation one can verify that ¯σ satisfies the Leibniz formula
(2.16). Thus, (r+(M∗), ¯σ) is a filtered differential graded Fp-algebra.
Lemma 9.1 ensures that ¯σ is also morphism in the category of filtered right A -
modules, and the proposition follows for (r+(M∗), ¯σ). By the discussion following
Proposition 2.13, the completed object (R+(M∗), ¯σ) is therefore a complete filtered
differential graded right A -module algebra.
□
Lemma 9.3. The image and the kernel of the differential ¯σ: Sr+(M∗) →sh1r+(M∗)

(9.7)
ker(¯σ) = im(¯σ) = ⟨tr ⊗x −utr ⊗σ(x) | r ∈Z, x ∈M∗⟩.

right-hand equality of (9.7).
We proceed to show that im(¯σ) = ker(¯σ), first as A -modules, and then as filtered

∗(THH(B)tCp) is a
filtered differential graded right A -module algebra by Proposition 5.1. In Subsec-
tion 10.3 we refine ΘMU to a morphism of rbb filtered differential graded right
A -module algebras.

XtCp, i.e., the restriction map associated to {e} ⊂T.

We write H∗(M∗, σ) for the homology of M∗with respect to the differential
σ: SM∗→M∗.
Furthermore, let H∗(M∗, β∗) = ker β∗/ im β∗be the Margolis
homology of M∗with respect to the Bockstein.
Lemma 10.12. The short exact sequence (10.16) splits as right A -modules only if
H∗(M∗, σ) = 0.
Proof. By an application of Lemma 9.4, the Margolis homology of C+(M∗; σ) with
respect to β∗is isomorphic to C+(H∗(M∗, σ); 0), which is trivial if and only if
H∗(M∗, σ) is trivial.
A splitting of (10.16) as right A -modules would imply that H∗(C+(M∗; σ), β∗)
splits off H∗(R+(M∗), β∗). However, the latter is trivial since the kernel and image
of β∗: R+(M∗) →R+(M∗) are both equal to the completion of the vector subspace
spanned by the elements of the form tr ⊗x, again by Lemma 9.4.
□
